=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given positive integers N, M, K, and a sequence of non-negative integers: A=(A_1,A_2,\ldots,A_N).
For a non-empty non-negative integer sequence B=(B_1,B_2,\ldots,B_{|B|}), we define its score as follows.

- If the length of B is a multiple of M: (B_1 \oplus B_2 \oplus \dots \oplus B_{|B|})^K
- Otherwise: 0

Here, \oplus represents the bitwise XOR.
Find the sum, modulo 998244353, of the scores of the 2^N-1 non-empty subsequences of A.
What is bitwise XOR? The bitwise XOR of non-negative integers A and B, denoted as A \oplus B, is defined as follows: - In the binary representation of A \oplus B, the digit at position 2^k (k \geq 0) is 1 if exactly one of A and B has a 1 in that position in their binary representations, and 0 otherwise. For example, 3 \oplus 5 = 6 (in binary: 011 \oplus 101 = 110). In general, the XOR of k integers p_1, \dots, p_k is defined as (\cdots ((p_1 \oplus p_2) \oplus p_3) \oplus \cdots \oplus p_k), and it can be proved that this is independent of the order of p_1, \dots, p_k.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M K
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N,K \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq M \leq 100
- 0 \leq A_i < 2^{20}
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 2 2
1 2 3

Sample Output 1

14

Here are the scores of the 2^3-1=7 non-empty subsequences of A.

- (1): 0
- (2): 0
- (3): 0
- (1,2): (1\oplus2)^2=9
- (1,3): (1\oplus3)^2=4
- (2,3): (2\oplus3)^2=1
- (1,2,3): 0

Therefore, the sought sum is 0+0+0+9+4+1+0=14.

Sample Input 2

10 5 3
100 100 100 100 100 100 100 100 100 100

Sample Output 2

252000000

Sample Input 3

16 4 100
7053 3876 3178 8422 7802 5998 2334 6757 6889 6637 7365 9495 7848 9026 7312 6558

Sample Output 3

432440016

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's inspect modulo z^M-1. Equation (1-z)P_{n+1} = (1+z)P_n. Since 1-z has kernel spanned by constant polynomial (all ones vector). Thus any two solutions differ by c * (1,1,...,1) (constant vector). So P_{n+1} is determined up to adding constant c to all coefficients. Need determine c. Maybe use another relation from evaluating at z=-1? If M even, z=-1 root? Equation at z=-1: (1-(-1))P_{n+1}(-1)=2P_{n+1}(-1) = (1-1)P_n(-1)=0, so P_{n+1}(-1)=0, which is automatic? P_{n+1}(-1)=0 if n+1>0. That gives sum (-1)^k p_{n+1}[k]=0. This can determine c. If M even. For general M, maybe use derivative at z=1? Since P_{n+1} has zero of order N-n-1 at z=1, P_n zero order N-n. Equation has factor. The derivative of equation at z=1:
(-1) P_{n+1}(1) + (1-z)P'_{n+1}(1) = P_n(1)+P_n'(1)? At z=1: -P_{n+1}(1) = 2 P_n(1) +? Wait derivative of RHS (1+z)P_n: P_n + (1+z)P_n'. At z=1: 2 P_n'(1). LHS derivative of (1-z)P_{n+1}: -P_{n+1} + (1-z)P_{n+1}'. At z=1: -P_{n+1}(1). So -P_{n+1}(1) = 2 P_n'(1). Since P_{n+1}(1)=0 if n+1<N, this gives P_n'(1)=0 if n<N-1, true. Not enough.

Maybe use derivatives up to order? Could determine c via sum of coefficients? Sum P_{n+1}(1)=0 if n+1<N. In vector terms sum p_{n+1}[k]=0. This is a linear condition. Does it determine c? Let's check. Suppose p is a solution to (I-S)p = q. Adding c*1 changes sum by M c. If M not divisible by MOD (M<MOD), sum condition determines c. But is sum p_{n+1}=P_{n+1}(1) equal 0? For n+1<N, yes P_{n+1}(1)=0. For n+1=N, P_N(1)=2^N. For n=N? no transition. So yes! This may allow recurrence! Great.

Let's formalize.

Let p_n be length M vector of coefficients of P_n(z) = (1+z)^n(1-z)^{N-n} modulo z^M-1, i.e., p_n[r] = sum_{q≥0} coeff degree r+qM. We need p_n[0].

Relation:
(1-z) P_{n+1} = (1+z) P_n.
In cyclic indices mod M:
p_{n+1}[r] - p_{n+1}[r-1] = p_n[r] + p_n[r-1] (with indices mod M). Let S be cyclic shift: (S v)[r] = v[r-1]. Then (I - S) p_{n+1} = (I + S) p_n.

We can solve for p_{n+1} given p_n plus sum condition. Since kernel of I-S is constants. We can choose a particular solution and adjust constant so sum p_{n+1} = P_{n+1}(1). What is P_{n+1}(1)? If n+1 < N, 0. If n+1 = N, 2^N. But we only need n from 0 to N-1 to compute p_N? Actually need C(n) for n=0..N. We can start p_0 = (1-z)^N mod z^M-1. Then update to p_n. Need p_0 known. Then for n=0..N maybe C(n)=p_n[0]. We can compute p_0 via binomial coefficients mod M? (1-z)^N = ∑_{r=0}^N (-1)^r C(N,r) z^r; reduce mod z^M-1. Need compute p_0 length M in O(N) or O(M+N). Then iterate n=0..N-1 using recurrence. Complexity O(N*M) if solving each step O(M). Need derive efficient solve.

Equation (I-S) p_{n+1} = q = (I+S) p_n.

Let's denote v = p_{n+1}. We need find v such that v_r - v_{r-1} = q_r for r=0..M-1 (indices mod M). Summing over r gives ∑ q_r = 0 necessary. Does q=(I+S)p_n have sum = ∑ p_n + ∑ S p_n = 2 sum p_n. Is sum p_n = P_n(1). For n<N, P_n(1)=0, so sum q=0. For n=N? no update. For n=N? not needed. For n=N-1, p_n sum = P_{N-1}(1)=0, q sum 0. Good. So consistent.

Given q with sum 0, solutions v satisfy differences. Let choose v_0 = t. Then for r=1..M-1:
v_r = v_{r-1} + q_r = t + ∑_{i=1}^r q_i.
For r=0, equation v_0 - v_{M-1} = q_0. This imposes t - (t + ∑_{i=1}^{M-1} q_i) = q_0 => -∑_{i=1}^{M-1} q_i = q_0 => ∑_{i=0}^{M-1} q_i=0, okay.

So v_r = t + prefix_q[r], where prefix_q[0]=0? Let's define pref[r] = ∑_{i=1}^r q_i for r>=1, pref[0]=0. Then v_r = t + pref[r]. (Check r=0: v0=t+0). Need determine t using sum condition:
∑_{r=0}^{M-1} v_r = M t + ∑_{r=0}^{M-1} pref[r] = target_sum = P_{n+1}(1).
Thus t = (target_sum - sum_pref) / M mod MOD. Since M invertible.

Great! So update O(M): compute q_r = p_n[r] + p_n[r-1] (since (I+S)p: q_r = p_r + p_{r-1}). Then prefix sums. Then v. Need target_sum = P_{n+1}(1) = 2^{n+1} * 0^{N-n-1}. For n+1 < N, target=0. For n+1 = N, target=2^N. But wait if N-n-1=0, P_N(1)=2^N. Yes. For n+1<N, zero. What if N=0? N>=1. For n=N-1, target=2^N. For n<N-1, target=0. We can compute target = pow2[N] if n+1==N else 0. But note p_n sum for n<N is 0; p_N sum=2^N. Good.

FWHT implementation:
```
def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            for j in range(i, i+h):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j+h]
                a[j] = (x + y) % MOD
                a[j+h] = (x - y) % MOD
        h = step
```
This is standard. But Python nested loops 20M; modulo each assignment. Could optimize by using local variables:
```
mod=MOD
for i in range(0,n,step):
    j=i; end=i+h
    while j<end:
        x=a[j]; y=a[j+h]
        a[j]=x+y
        if a[j]>=mod: a[j]-=mod
        a[j+h]=x-y
        if a[j+h]<0: a[j+h]+=mod
        j+=1
```
Since x,y in [0,MOD), x+y <2MOD, so one subtraction enough. x-y in (-MOD,MOD), one addition enough. This avoids modulo. Need ensure after each stage entries remain in [0,MOD). Yes. Use while loops maybe faster than for? For Python, for range loop often faster than while? Let's consider. Standard:
```
for j in range(i, i+h):
    x = a[j]; y = a[j+h]
    u = x + y
    if u >= MOD: u -= MOD
    v = x - y
    if v < 0: v += MOD
    a[j] = u; a[j+h] = v
```
This is okay. 20M iterations, each with ifs. Might be ~3s per transform, two transforms ~6s. Need maybe optimize further.

Now, FWHT implementation details and performance.

Let's write `fwht(a)`:

```
def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    mod = MOD
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            end = i + h
            j = i
            while j < end:
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                u = x + y
                if u >= mod:
                    u -= mod
                v = x - y
                if v < 0:
                    v += mod
                a[j] = u
                a[j + h] = v
                j += 1
        h = step
```
Using while j loop might be slower than for j in range? In PyPy, for range loops are optimized; while also. Let's use for:
```
for j in range(i, i+h):
    x = a[j]; y = a[j+h]
    ...
```
Range object creation per block? There are many blocks: total blocks across stages = G-1? Actually number of blocks = sum_{h=1}^{G/2} G/(2h) = G-1 ~1e6. Creating range for each block overhead. While loop avoids range object? It still has while. Maybe for j in range is implemented in C for iteration, faster. But creating many range objects (1e6) may cost. Could use while to avoid range object? In Python, for range is efficient but range object creation is cheap? 1e6 range objects maybe not cheap. Let's think: total blocks over all stages = G/2 + G/4 + ... +1 = G-1 ~1,048,575. That's 1 million range objects. Could be significant. While loop with manual j avoids range object but Python while condition and increment. Which is faster? Hard. Common FWHT Python implementations use:
```
for i in range(0,n,step):
    for j in range(i,i+h):
        ...
```
Often accepted for 2^20 in PyPy? Maybe.

We can optimize by processing using `for j in range(i, i+h):` and local variables. Let's stick.

Could use `for j in range(h): x=a[i+j]; y=a[i+j+h]` maybe similar.

Maybe use `itertools`? no.

n=1: q1=0, target=4, t=2, p2=[2,2], C2=2. Good.

Let's think about p0 computation if M=1 and N large. p0 expansion O(N) but p0[0] = 0. Could skip? Not necessary.

Now, let's consider input size. N up to 2e5, reading A. Use sys.stdin.buffer.read().split().

Implementation outline:

```
import sys
MOD = 998244353

def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    mod = MOD
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            end = i + h
            for j in range(i, end):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                u = x + y
                if u >= mod:
                    u -= mod
                v = x - y
                if v < 0:
                    v += mod
                a[j] = u
                a[j + h] = v
        h = step

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, M, K = data[0], data[1], data[2]
    A = data[3:]
    # precompute inv up to N
    inv = [0]*(N+2)
    if N+1 >= 1:
        inv[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, N+2):
        inv[i] = MOD - (MOD//i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD
    # p0
    p = [0]*M
    comb = 1
    for r in range(N+1):
        idx = r % M
        if r & 1:
            p[idx] -= comb
        else:
            p[idx] += comb
        if r < N:
            comb = comb * (N - r) % MOD * inv[r+1] % MOD
    for i in range(M):
        p[i] %= MOD
    C_list = [0]*(N+1)
    C_list[0] = p[0]
    invM = pow(M, MOD-2, MOD)
    pow2N = pow(2, N, MOD)
    pref = [0]*M
    newp = [0]*M
    for n in range(N):
        s = 0
        sum_pref = 0
        pref[0] = 0
        # compute q_r = p[r] + p[r-1] for r=1..M-1
        for r in range(1, M):
            s += p[r] + p[r-1]
            pref[r] = s
            sum_pref += s
        target = pow2N if n == N-1 else 0
        t = (target - sum_pref) * invM % MOD
        newp[0] = t
        for r in range(1, M):
            newp[r] = (t + pref[r]) % MOD
        p, newp = newp, p
        C_list[n+1] = p[0]
    # determine G
    max_a = max(A) if A else 0
    L = max_a.bit_length()
    G = 1 << L
    # cnt
    cnt = [0]*G
    for a in A:
        cnt[a] += 1
    fwht(cnt)
    # replace with C_a
    Nmod = N
    for i in range(G):
        val = cnt[i]
        if val > Nmod:
            h = val - MOD
        else:
            h = val
        n = (Nmod + h) // 2
        cnt[i] = C_list[n]
    fwht(cnt)
    invG = pow(G, MOD-2, MOD)
    ans = 0
    mod = MOD
    # dot with x^K
    for x in range(G):
        c = cnt[x]
        if c:
            ans = (ans + c * pow(x, K, mod)) % mod
    ans = ans * invG % mod
    print(ans)
```

Now, FWHT can also be optimized with local variables and ifs. Let's write:

```
def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    mod = MOD
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            end = i + h
            for j in range(i, end):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                u = x + y
                if u >= mod:
                    u -= mod
                v = x - y
                if v < 0:
                    v += mod
                a[j] = u
                a[j + h] = v
        h = step
```
Could use `while j < end` to avoid range object? Let's decide. For PyPy, `for j in range(i, end)` is usually faster because range iteration in C? Actually PyPy optimizes loops. We'll use for.

Maybe we can optimize by using local `aa = a`? Already local. `mod` local.

Could use `for j in range(i, i + h):` instead of end. Fine.

Now, after first FWHT, cnt values are H_a mod MOD. We replace with C_list. But if G maybe 1, fwht no loops. Good.

Now, final dot: We can optimize by precomputing powers for x only if cnt[x] nonzero? Need pow for each nonzero. Could use list of nonzero indices? After second FWHT, cnt may have many zeros? Not necessarily. But we can iterate and skip c==0. If c==0, no pow. Good.

        if ans >= (1 << 63):
            ans %= mod

ans = ans * invG % mod
```

    if c:
        ans += c * pow(x, K, MOD)

```
This avoids 1e6 modulo operations. Great. 1e24 fits in Python int easily. Let's use this. Need ensure c maybe 0; skip. If all c nonzero, 1e6 multiplications and additions. Good.

We have P_n = (1+z)^n(1-z)^{N-n}. For n from 0 to N-1:
(1-z)P_{n+1} = (1-z)(1+z)^{n+1}(1-z)^{N-n-1} = (1+z)^{n+1}(1-z)^{N-n} = (1+z)(1+z)^n(1-z)^{N-n} = (1+z)P_n. Yes.

In ring mod z^M-1, multiplication by z is cyclic shift S: (S v)[r] = v[(r-1) mod M]? Let's define coefficient of z^r in z P(z) is coefficient of z^{r-1} in P, i.e., p[(r-1) mod M]. So (zP)[r] = p[r-1]. Then (1-z)P: p[r] - p[r-1]. (1+z)P: p[r] + p[r-1]. Equation (I-S)p_{n+1} = (I+S)p_n. Good.

Let v=p_{n+1}, q=(I+S)p_n. Then v_r - v_{r-1}=q_r. We set v0=t. For r=1: v1 - v0 = q1 => v1=t+q1. For r=2: v2 - v1 = q2 => v2=t+q1+q2. So v_r = t + pref[r], pref[0]=0, pref[r]=∑_{i=1}^r q_i. Good.

Sum v = M t + ∑_{r=0}^{M-1} pref[r]. Target = P_{n+1}(1). What is P_{n+1}(1) in ring? P_{n+1}(1) = (1+1)^{n+1}(1-1)^{N-n-1}. If N-n-1 >0, 0. If N-n-1=0 (n=N-1), 2^N. If N-n-1<0 no. So target as above. Good.

Now, q sum zero? ∑_{r=0}^{M-1} q_r = ∑ (p_r + p_{r-1}) = 2∑p_r = 2 P_n(1). For n<N, P_n(1)=0 because N-n>0. For n=N-1, P_{N-1}(1)=0. So q sum zero. This ensures equation for r=0 is consistent. We don't use q0. If due to modulo, q sum zero mod MOD. Good.

Now, let's think about if `fwht` can be optimized by using `while` to avoid range object. Let's decide final code. Many competitive Python FWHT implementations use:
```
def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    while h < n:
        for i in range(0, n, h*2):
            for j in range(i, i+h):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j+h]
                a[j] = (x+y)%MOD
                a[j+h] = (x-y)%MOD
        h *= 2
```
This is accepted for 2^20 in PyPy maybe. Our optimized ifs better.

Could use `while j < end` to avoid creating range object for each block. Let's see: 1e6 range objects might be significant. But `for j in range(i, end)` creates a range object and iterates in C? Actually `range` object creation is cheap but 1e6 times maybe not. The inner loop total 20M iterations; block overhead 1e6. Using while also has Python-level condition/increment for each iteration, while for-range uses C for next? In CPython, for loop over range still increments Python int but in C? The iterator's next returns Python int, but loop overhead in C? The body executes Python bytecode. While loop has more bytecode (compare, increment). For-range is generally faster. Keep for-range.

```
def main():
    data=...
    N,M,K=...
    A=data[3:3+N]
    del data
    # compute C_list
    ...
    # compute G and cnt
    max_a = max(A)
    G = 1 << max_a.bit_length()
    cnt = [0]*G
    for a in A: cnt[a]+=1
    del A
    fwht(cnt)
    ...
```
This computes C_list before cnt. During C_list, A memory held. Fine. Could compute cnt before C_list to maybe use A and then delete before C_list? But C_list doesn't need A, but cnt memory plus C_list. Either. Maybe compute cnt first then delete A, then C_list, to reduce memory during C_list? C_list small. But FWHT needs cnt; C_list computation before FWHT okay. If compute cnt first, then C_list, then FWHT, memory has cnt and A? We can delete A after cnt. Then compute C_list. That may reduce peak? Peak during cnt build: data? We deleted data, A and cnt. Then delete A. Then C_list. Peak includes cnt + A + C_list. If compute C_list first, peak includes A + C_list + p small, then cnt + C_list. Similar. No matter.

Maybe compute C_list first because it uses N,M and can be done while data still? But A slice. Let's do C_list first then cnt to keep code linear.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with final answer if `G` is not the full 2^20 but max bit length; the inverse transform over smaller group is valid. Let's prove: Let L = max bit length. All A_i and XORs are in H = {0,...,2^L-1}. The full group G_full = (Z2)^20. Characters with masks a_full = a_low + a_high. For any A_i in H, χ_{a_full}(A_i) = χ_{a_low}(A_i). Thus C_{a_full} depends only on a_low. There are 2^{20-L} characters for each a_low. In inverse over full group:
C0[x] = 1/2^20 ∑_{a_low} C_{a_low} ∑_{a_high} χ_{a_low}(x) (χ_{a_high}(x)=1 because x in H) = 2^{20-L}/2^20 ∑_{a_low} C_{a_low} χ_{a_low}(x) = 1/2^L ∑_{a_low} ... . So using G=2^L and invG works. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
with code.</think>```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353


def fwht(a):
    n = len(a)
    h = 1
    mod = MOD
    while h < n:
        step = h << 1
        for i in range(0, n, step):
            end = i + h
            for j in range(i, end):
                x = a[j]
                y = a[j + h]
                u = x + y
                if u >= mod:
                    u -= mod
                v = x - y
                if v < 0:
                    v += mod
                a[j] = u
                a[j + h] = v
        h = step


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N, M, K = data[0], data[1], data[2]
    A = data[3:3 + N]
    del data

    # modular inverses up to N
    inv = [0] * (N + 2)
    inv[1] = 1
    for i in range(2, N + 2):
        inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

    # p0 = (1 - z)^N mod (z^M - 1)
    p = [0] * M
    comb = 1
    idx = 0
    for r in range(N + 1):
        if r & 1:
            p[idx] -= comb
        else:
            p[idx] += comb
        if r < N:
            comb = comb * (N - r) % MOD * inv[r + 1] % MOD
        idx += 1
        if idx == M:
            idx = 0
    for i in range(M):
        p[i] %= MOD

    # C_list[n] = sum of coefficients with degree multiple of M in
    # (1+z)^n (1-z)^(N-n)
    C_list = [0] * (N + 1)
    C_list[0] = p[0]

    invM = pow(M, MOD - 2, MOD)
    pow2N = pow(2, N, MOD)

    pref = [0] * M
    newp = [0] * M

    for n in range(N):
        s = 0
        sum_pref = 0
        for r in range(1, M):
            s += p[r] + p[r - 1]
            if s >= MOD:
                s -= MOD
                if s >= MOD:
                    s -= MOD
            pref[r] = s
            sum_pref += s

        target = pow2N if n == N - 1 else 0
        t = ((target - sum_pref) % MOD) * invM % MOD

        newp[0] = t
        for r in range(1, M):
            v = t + pref[r]
            if v >= MOD:
                v -= MOD
            newp[r] = v

        p, newp = newp, p
        C_list[n + 1] = p[0]

    # Use only bits appearing in A
    max_a = max(A)
    G = 1 << max_a.bit_length()

    cnt = [0] * G
    for a in A:
        cnt[a] += 1
    del A

    # Walsh-Hadamard transform of counts
    fwht(cnt)

    # cnt[a] is now H_a = sum_i chi_a(A_i)
    for i in range(G):
        val = cnt[i]
        if val > N:
            h = val - MOD
        else:
            h = val
        n = (N + h) // 2
        cnt[i] = C_list[n]

    del C_list

    # Inverse transform: cnt[x] becomes G * (number of valid subsequences with XOR x)
    fwht(cnt)

    invG = pow(G, MOD - 2, MOD)

    ans = 0
    pow_mod = pow
    mod = MOD
    for x in range(1, G):
        c = cnt[x]
        if c:
            ans += c * pow_mod(x, K, mod)

    ans %= mod
    ans = ans * invG % mod
    print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
